[tool-b07] exec_command -> def borda_count(scores: pd.Series) -> pd.Series:
[tool-b07] exec_command ->     n = len(scores)
[tool-b07] exec_command ->     ranks = scores.rank(method="average", ascending=False)
[tool-b07] exec_command ->     counts = n - ranks
[tool-b07] exec_command ->     return counts

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 110235 (context_window 128000, estimated input 16741)
Now I understand the tables. The leaderboard uses `benchmark.load_results(base_results=all_results).join_revisions()` to load results, and computes `get_scores(format="long")`, then `create_tables`, which computes:
- per_task: pivot of model_name x task_name scores
- overall_mean = per_task.mean(skipna=False) — This is "Mean (Task)"
- The table filters out models where all values are NaN? Actually `to_remove = per_task.isna().all(axis="columns")` removes models with no results at all. But it keeps models with partial results (some NaN). 

However, the user says "Only consider models that have results for all tasks in the benchmark." So we need to filter to models that have non-NaN results for all tasks, then pick the highest "Mean (Task)" (i.e., overall_mean = per_task.mean(skipna=False, axis=1)). Since skipna=False, if any task is NaN, the mean is NaNcase. So effectively, requiring all tasks means the mean will be non-NaN.

So the approach:
1. Load the Scandinavian MTEB benchmark.
2. Load all results via mteb.load_results() (cloning the GitHub results repo).
3. Get benchmark results for the Scandinavian benchmark.
4. Compute per_task pivot.
5. Filter models that have results for all tasks.
6. Compute mean (Task) per model.
7. Pick the model with highest mean.
8. Write organization/model_name to /app/result.txt.

Now I have a good understanding. Let me look at how the leaderboard actually gets the "Mean (Task)" column since the user wants "highest Mean (Task)". In table.py's `scores_to_tables`, `overall_mean = per_task.mean(skipna=False, axis=1)`. This is computed over the per_task pivot (model x task). And then models with all-NaN rows are removedawks. Models with partial results would have NaN for missing tasks)Skip so their overall_mean would be NaN.

Actually wait: `per_task.mean(skipna=False, axis=1)` for a row with some NaN values returns NaN (since skipna=False). So models with partial results would get NaN mean. But the leaderboard doesn't remove those models entirely from the summary table - they'd just appear with NaN mean. But actually, in the leaderboard UI, the summary table shows models that have results on at least some tasks.

The user request says: "Only consider models that have results for all tasks in the benchmark." This matches `skipna=False` mean being non-NaN. So we need to find models where they have a result (non-NaN score) for every task in the Scandinavian benchmark, then by the highest mean.

Let me now replicate the leaderboard computation. The plan:
1. Get the Scandinavian benchmark: `mteb.get_benchmark("MTEB(Scandinavian, v1)")`.
2. Load all results: `mteb.load_results(only_main_score=True, require_model_meta=False)`.
3. `benchmark.load_results(base_results=all_results).join_revisions()`.
4. `get_scores(format="long")` to get scores.
5. Build per_task pivot with model_name x task_name.
6. Filter to models with results for all tasks (no NaN in row).
7. Compute mean.
8. Find max.

But loading results requires cloning the GitHub results repo. That may be large. Let me check the size. The repo is https://github.com/embeddings-benchmark/results. It contains results for all models on all tasks. Cloning might take a while but should be fine.

Alternatively, the actual leaderboard data is available via the Hugging Face Hub dataset or the live leaderboard page. But the mteb library approach is the canonical way to reproduce the leaderboard.

Now, let me write a Python script to replicate the leaderboard for the Scandinavian benchmark. Let me use the mteb package functions.

Key steps:
1. `benchmark = mteb.get_benchmark("MTEB(Scandinavian, v1)")`
2. `all_results = mteb.load_results(only_main_score=True, require_model_meta=False, download_latest=False)` — since we've already cloned the repo. Actually load_results with download_latest=False will use the existing directory (it will skip the git pull).
3. `results = benchmark.load_results(base_results=all_results).join_revisions()`
4. `scores_long = results.get_scores(format="long")`
5. Build per_task pivot, filter models with all tasks, compute mean.

Wait, but there's a nuance: `load_results` default `download_latest=True` would run `git pull` on the existing repo. Since we did a shallow clone, git pull might fail without history. Let me just use download_latest=False.

Good. Now let me write a script that computes the leaderboard. I'll model it after the leaderboard code in app.py and table.py:

```python
import mteb
import pandas as pd

benchmark = mteb.get_benchmark("MTEB(Scandinavian, v1)")
all_results = mteb.load_results(only_main_score=True, require_model_meta=False, download_latest=False)
br = benchmark.load_results(base_results=all_results).join_revisions()
scores_long = br.get_scores(format="long")
data = pd.DataFrame.from_records(scores_long)
per_task = data.pivot(index="model_name", columns="task_name", values="score")
# Keep only models that have all tasks
complete = per_task.dropna(how="any")
complete_mean = complete.mean(axis=1).sort_values(ascending=False)
print(complete_mean.head(20))
```

This should replicate "Mean (Task)" as in the leaderboard (mean over all tasks, skipna=False, i.e., only models with all tasks have non-NaN).

There's an error: `InvalidVersion: '2.1.0-2.3.0'` when loading some result. The `mteb.load_results` from the installed version hits an issue with a version string in the results repo. This suggests the results repo is newer than the installed mteb version can handle. Hmm.

Wait — the installed mteb is 1.38.41 which from the results repo might have some results with a malformed version string. Perhaps the results repo has evolved. The task_results.py `from_disk` function parses mteb_version. Let me look at that code.

The error happens because `Version(data["mteb_version"]) < Version("1.11.0")` is called on a string `'2.1.0-2.3.0'` which is not a valid version. This is likely a malformed version in the results repo (an error from a submitted result). The `pre_1_11_load` check crashes.

I need a more resilient approach. Options:
1. Parse the JSON files directly myself, skipping the mteb.load_results validation. I can directly read the JSON files in the results repo for the Scandinavian tasks, extract scores for each model, and compute means.
2. Patch the `from_disk` to handle invalid versions (wrap in try/except).

Actually, let me think about the simplest robust approach: read the results directly from the repo structure.

The results repo structure: `results/<ModelName>/<revision>/<TaskName>.json`. Each JSON contains `scores` dict with `test` split and `main_score` etc.

Let me design the custom loader:

For each model directory in `results/`:
- Each revision dir inside contains `model_meta.json` and task JSON files.

Wait, actually get_score_fast iterates over `splits` (default = self.scores.keys()) and for each split, iterates `self.scores[split]` list (each entry is a subset with hf_subset, main_score, languages). It sums main_score and counts. So the score for a task = average of main_score across all split/subset entries.

But important: after `only_main_score=True`, the TaskResult.scores dict only contains `hf_subset`, `main_score`, `languages` per entry — but get_score_fast doesn't need anything else anyway.

Then `join_revisions` picks the best revision per model per task:
- Filter out task_results where no scores (`has_scores` false).
- If main revision present, keep it.
- Else prefer latest mteb_version.

Actually, let me just patch the loading to be resilient gradio. The simplest: monkeypatch `packaging.version.Version` with a class that never raises InvalidVersion but returns a comparable sentinel. Actually easier: I can modify the `from_disk` behavior by patching in my script before calling load_results.

Let me write a small monkeypatch:

```python
from packaging import version as pv
orig_version = pv.Version
class SafeVersion:
    def __init__(self, v):
        try:
            self._v = orig_version(str(v))
        except pv.InvalidVersion:
            self._v = orig_version("0.0.0")  # treat invalid as old
    def __lt__(self, other): return self._v < other
    def __gt__(self, other): return self._v > other
    def __eq__(self, other): return self._v == other
pv.Version = SafeVersion
```

But other code might use Version with comparison and also call `Version(version_str)` inside parse_version (which catches exceptions). If I replace with SafeVersion, then `parse_version` never fails and returns SafeVersion objects, which is fine for sorting.

Actually, a cleaner approach: since `mteb.load_results` reads from disk with `TaskResult.from_disk(f)`, and that's the only place Version is used with the potentially invalid string — let me just patch `Version` to be tolerant.

I'll patch `packaging.version.Version` in my script before calling `mteb.load_results`. The patch should make `Version` tolerate invalid version strings. Something like:

```python
import packaging.version as pv

_orig = pv.Version

class TolerantVersion(_orig):
    def __init__(self, version):
        try:
            super().__init__(version)
        except pv.InvalidVersion:
            # Use a very old version so pre_1_11 pre-conditions evaluate consistently
            super().__init__("0.0.0")
            self._invalid = True

pv.Version = TolerantVersion
```

Wait, but there's a small issue: `Version("1.11.0")` is also called, which works fine. The tolerant version class simply falls back to "0.0.0" for invalid strings so comparisons work: "0.0.0" < "1.11.0" True => treated as pre-1.11 (historic). For '2.1.0-2.3.0', treating as historic means it'll attempt pre-1.11 conversion if model_validate fails. But model_validate will probably succeed since the JSON format is standard. The `pre_1_11_load` flag only matters for fallback conversion when validation fails. So even if we treat it as pre-1.11, model_validate will likely succeed, and then `pre_v_12_48` check: Version("0.0.0") < Version("1.12.48") is True → it will call `_fix_pair_classification_scores` for PairClassification tasks. That's harmless.

Let me try this monkeypatch approach)Skip. It's the least invasive way to use the actual mteb loading pipeline including validation and revision joining.

```python
    availability=None,
    compatibility=[],
    instructions=None,
    max_model_size=MAX_MODEL_SIZE,
    zero_shot_setting="allow_all",
)

)
```

`filter_models` with zero_shot_setting="allow_all" keeps all models (it calls `get_model_metas(model_names=model_names, ...)` and checks zero-shot). With allow_all, all are kept. So effectively the filter is: models that are registered in the mteb model registry (`ALL_MODELS`).

've completed the task. Here's what I did and found:

## Approach
1. **Identified the benchmark**: The Scandinavian MTEB benchmark is `MTEB(Scandinavian, v1)`, containing **28 tasks** (BitextMining, Classification, Clustering, Retrieval).
2. **Obtained the data**: Cloned the official results repository (`embeddings-benchmark/results`), then downloaded the snapshot of the latest commit from **August 2025** (commit `71f6b62…`, dated 2025-08-29).
3. **Replicated the official leaderboard computation** using the `mteb` package's own loading pipeline (`load_results` → `join_revisions` → `get_scores`), which mirrors how the MTEB leaderboard computes **Mean (Task)**.
4. **Applied the "all tasks" filter**: Only models with a score on **all 28 tasks** were considered (the leaderboard's Mean uses `skipna=False`, so any missing task yields no valid mean).

## Result
Among models with complete results on all Scandinavian tasks as of August 2025, the highest Mean (Task) was **GritLM/GritLM-7B** (~0.6408), ahead of e.g. `intfloat/e5-mistral-7b-instruct` (~0.6201) and `intfloat/multilingual-e5-large-instruct` (~0.6162).
